#7206. Dentsu makes entertainment and sport a C-suite priority amid earnings stumble

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Expertise in entertainment could be the next agency C-suite superpower. Takeshi Sano, the president and global CEO of Japanese agency holding company Dentsu, clearly thinks so: he’s elevated Mitsuyuki ‘Mike’ Nakamura, a former media buyer, TV producer and a 36-year Dentsu veteran, to serve as the group’s first global practice president for sports and entertainment.

Nakamura’s appointment is further proof of the growing importance of entertainment expertise to major ad agencies,  as media spend concentrates around live sports and TV, film and showbiz reknit around the creator economy.

“Sports and entertainment is where culture is made today, and Dentsu has a long track-record of helping brands go further than just buying space around it,” said Sano in a statement.

Nakamura has worked for Dentsu in both Thailand, as CEO of Dentsu Media, and the U.K., where he led its CX business, Dentsu X. Most recently he served as president of sports and entertainment at Dentsu Japan.

Dentsu is more closely coiled with the entertainment sector than most advertising groups. It’s an investor in the upcoming Angry Birds film, which will also feature two of its clients. It has produced microdramas for P&G and recently inked a partnership with Iconic Arts to help brands create their own anime. It’s not alone in moving closer to the showbiz world, though; last week, Ogilvy launched a partnership with top talent agency CAA to use AI clones of its high profile stars in brand advertising.

The company made the announcement the same day that it released its third-quarter earnings report. Overall organic revenue fell 3.7% and the company altered its revenue guidance for the whole of 2026 from 0.1% growth to -1%.

Organic growth reversed in Japan, the Americas (down 6.5%) and Europe, the Middle East and Africa (EMEA), where income fell 7.1% due to “macro uncertainty and reduced business from major clients” hitting media revenues. Dentsu’s media revenues in the Americas have fallen 2.5% this year so far.

The company’s results come amid signs of a potential slowdown in overall global advertising spending. Amazon, Google and Reddit each recorded deceleration in advertising revenues this past quarter; the former saw Amazon ad revenue growth fall from 26% in Q2, to 12% in Q3.

But Dentsu has fared worse than its major agency group rivals. WPP’s revenue for the same period was up 0.5%, while Publicis’ rose 5%.

And Brian Wieser, founder of Madison & Wall, suggested its revenue declines reflect its own circumstances, rather than a broader slowdown – namely the absence of Microsoft from its balance sheet. The company lost the tech giant’s $2.67 billion global account to Publicis back in April.

“Its scale should not be underestimated,” Wieser said. “It was probably by far the biggest client that Dentsu had.”

Spending on ad agencies is increasingly divorced from spending on advertising as a whole, Wieser added. “Marketers have been so focused on containing their non-working spending, as they characterize it, that the growth of spend on [advertising] is independent of the growth of spend on agencies,” he said.

The bottom line is that Dentsu can’t rely on the ad spend tides to raise its boat, but must find its own way back to growth.

#7203. Ad Tech Briefing: Google’s antitrust troubles aren’t over

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

Developments last week showed that Google faces another potential antitrust setback, this time in Europe, as it prepares for the next stage of its U.S. ad tech monopoly case.

On Nov. 12, an adviser to the European Union’s highest court recommended overturning a 2024 judgment that annulled an earlier €1.49 billion ($1.7 billion) fine against Google over its AdSense for Search business.

In her ⁠opinion, Advocate General Juliane Kokott argued that the EU’s General Court made legal errors when it overturned the European Commission’s original 2019 penalty. Specifically, Kokott concluded that the lower court had imposed excessive requirements on regulators when assessing whether Google’s contractual restrictions harmed competition.

The case concerns agreements Google used with publishers that displayed search advertisements through its AdSense for Search service; i.e., it involves a different business unit than the one under scrutiny in its most recent U.S. trial.

Per the European proceedings’ plaintiffs, between 2006 and 2016, Google imposed contractual restrictions that limited those websites’ ability to display advertisements from rival search advertising providers.

The European Commission identified three types of restrictions: exclusivity clauses preventing publishers from using competing services, placement clauses restricting where rival advertisements could appear, and provisions requiring Google’s prior approval before changes to advertising displays.

The Commission concluded that these practices constituted a single, continuous infringement of European competition law. Google challenged the decision, and in September 2024, the General Court annulled the fine, finding that regulators had failed to examine relevant circumstances adequately, particularly the duration of Google’s contractual arrangements.

Kokott’s opinion, published Nov. 12, argued that the General Court had placed excessive emphasis on the duration of individual agreements rather than considering their collective effect on competition.

In the accompanying press release, the court explained that regulators could assess contracts collectively to determine whether they could produce exclusionary effects. Kokott recommended setting aside the 2024 judgment and returning the case to the General Court for reconsideration.

Importantly, the recommendation does not automatically reinstate the original fine. The opinion is nonbinding, and the Court of Justice has yet to issue its judgment. Moreover, Kokott acknowledged that the penalty should ultimately be reduced because certain findings concerning Google’s conduct in 2016 are no longer being contested.

Nevertheless, the opinion creates the prospect of another prolonged legal battle over Google’s advertising practices, as the development comes two months after Google avoided a forced breakup of its advertising technology business in the U.S.

In September, federal judge Leonie Brinkema rejected the Justice Department’s proposed divestiture of Google’s AdX exchange and publisher ad server, DoubleClick for Publishers.

Instead, the court accepted most of the government’s proposed behavioral remedies, subject to modifications. The decision shifted the focus from Google’s ownership of its advertising infrastructure to the restrictions governing how that infrastructure operates.

The remedies are intended to address Google’s ability to favor its own advertising products, including through changes affecting interoperability, auction transparency and access for competing ad tech companies. However, the precise obligations and their enforcement remain central questions as the parties work through the final judgment.

Google has indicated that it intends to appeal the underlying liability decision, potentially extending the litigation beyond implementation of the behavioral remedies.

The European and American cases concern different markets and legal questions, but both illustrate the difficulty regulators face in challenging Google’s advertising dominance.

In Europe, the courts are still examining conduct that ended a decade ago. In the U.S., the argument has moved from whether Google violated competition law to whether the remedies will materially change market behavior.

However, even successful antitrust enforcement does not guarantee that publishers and advertisers will move their business away from Google.

Meanwhile, last week’s proceedings demonstrated how The Trade Desk’s struggles highlight a different problem — a development that embodies the scale of the challenges independent competitors to Google, etc., face when challenging Big Tech.

On Nov. 9, The Trade Desk reported a rare quarterly revenue decline, with third-quarter sales falling approximately 0.5% to $736 million from $739 million a year earlier. While the company did beat Wall Street’s (modest) expectations, the result marked a sharp reversal for a company that had long delivered double-digit growth.

The results underscored the difficulties facing the company during what executives have described as a transitional year. The slowdown comes amid growing competition from Amazon, shifting agency relationships and the increasing use of AI to automate media buying.

While The Trade Desk has invested heavily in its Kokai platform and related AI capabilities, those initiatives have yet to restore its historical growth rates, with executives there also revealing how they are reconsidering how acquisitions might contribute to its next phase of growth.

On the company’s earnings call, Jeff Green, The Trade Desk, CEO, revealed leadership were taking a closer look at potential acquisitions, suggesting a more active approach to M&A than the company has historically pursued.

For years, The Trade Desk positioned itself as an independent alternative to the advertising ecosystems operated by Google, Amazon, and other major technology companies, with this pitch a key selling point when articulating its value in the responsible use of CMOs’ media investments.

However, its latest results demonstrate that independence alone is not enough to guarantee growth.

Regulators may eventually make Google’s advertising infrastructure more accessible to competitors. But those competitors must still persuade advertisers that their platforms offer better technology, performance, and economics. The Trade Desk’s difficulties suggest that the industry’s competitive challenges extend well beyond Google’s market power.

#7202. Holiday TV ads from retailers arrive earlier, more frequently in 2026

While retail historically commands the season, it accounted for 63.7% of holiday ads in October, pacing ahead of recent years.

The holidays are always a busy time for advertising, and Nielsen’s data show activity moving further up the calendar as brands try to make the most of a packed Q4. More campaigns are starting as early as September, with holiday creative this year accounting for 1% of all linear TV ads during the month. Even as ad-supported streaming expands, linear remains on a growth trajectory after surpassing $1 billion in spend in 2023, according to Nielsen.

Some elements of the holidays remain constant, including retail’s dominance, which is particularly pronounced so far this season. The category has commanded an average of 45% of linear TV ad spend over the past five years, and only a slightly smaller share of the burgeoning streaming environment. In October, 63.7% of holiday ads came from retailers.

Promotional messaging is a major theme, with over one-third of retail ads assessed by Nielsen over the past five years carrying mentions of Black Friday or Cyber Monday deals events. Retailers are currently under pressure to reach wary shoppers during a persistent run of inflation. Several campaigns have been themed around more thoughtful, personalized gifting instead of low prices alone.

Effective holiday strategies can boost performance, per Nielsen connected TV benchmark studies for 2025. Cosmetics brands recorded a 3.2 times sales lift compared to non-holiday campaigns while department stores experienced a 2.4 times sales lift, the measurement firm found. Restaurants and fast-food brands recorded sales lifts of 1.5 times and 1 times, respectively, versus non-holiday ads.

Outside of retail, category spending trends have fluctuated more noticeably over time. Automotive shifted from representing 16% of holiday linear TV ad spend in 2023 to just 7.7% in 2025. Nielsen attributed the retreat to a bigger focus on streaming, where auto makes up 18.4% of the market. A dearth of car commercials, once a fixture of prime time programming, has gained media attention as the industry navigates economic headwinds and the failure of electric vehicle bets to take off in the U.S.

Food and beverage advertising also dipped between 2024 and 2025, which Nielsen attributed to big brands tightening their budgets and challenger brands only running smaller experiments for the holidays. Large food and beverage marketers including Pepsi and Oreo have rolled out holiday efforts in the past few weeks, with the latter introducing its first new seasonal creative in five years.

#7200. What exactly counts as a creator? The industry still can’t agree

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Every brand seems to have different definitions when it comes to the creator economy. What one brand calls a tastemaker, another labels an influencer. If marketers can’t agree on creator taxonomy, standardization — on everything from measurement to cost — may remain further away than the industry would like.

Since the ballooning of the creator economy, marketers have been trying to standardize everything from what counts as a view, brand-creator contractual language and the framework for deal fee structures. According to the IAB’s latest creator economy report, at least 23% of marketers say the industry would benefit from a unified creator taxonomy.

“That’s the blurry line. Who’s a mega influencer and who’s a celebrity? I don’t know anymore,” said Mindy Murray, director of influencer and content at General Mills.

General Mills’ approach to creator definitions is nuanced. The highest tier is celebrity status, where partnerships often go beyond social and into product design and formula. Mega creators are positioned just below celebrity partnerships. Mid creators range from 500,000 to 1.5 million social media followers. There are super fans or brand advocates who are dedicated creators who are positing consistently on behalf of the brand. Finally, there’s friends and family, which is General Mills’ organic influencer group who also act as brand advocates.

Shutterfly has a three-tier creator codification. Tastemakers are social media stars with one million or more followers. They’re handpicked for major campaigns. Content creators fall on the other end of the spectrum, having anywhere from a couple hundred to more than 2,000 social media followers. Influencers fall somewhere in between, Callie Worthington, vp of brand marketing and creative at Shutterfly, told Digiday.

Shutterfly doesn’t have an internal pay scale that relates to its codification system. It is expected, however, that bigger creators (which could be tastemakers and influencers) command bigger budgets.

Rothy’s shoe brand also uses the term tastemakers. However, Rothy’s creator classifications are defined less by follower count and more by their cultural relevance, said Anna Doré, vp of brand and communications at Rothy’s. A tastemaker could be a TikTok star or a Substack writer. In comparison, Rothy’s refers to affiliate creators as “the lower funnel crater and conversion types,” Doré said.

Meanwhile, agencies don’t seem to go by the same guidelines. For example, VML organizes creators by their role in a campaign — salespeople, brand ambassadors, product collaborators, etc. The taxonomy isn’t as strict, Noah Eisemann, global executive director of creator and influencer at VML, told Digiday.

“Every influencer is a creator, but not every creator is an influencer,” Eisemann said.

The creator economy has exploded to where creators aren’t just selling products on behalf of brands. They’ve been hired as actors in Super Bowl spots. They’ve been brought on as product collaborators. Some are acting as creative agencies helping brands ideate and execute creative campaigns. Kim Kardashian is as much a content creator as she is an influencer, depending on what the brand asks of her, he added.

“The more useful standard may be a shared way of defining the role a creator is playing in a particular strategy — because that’s ultimately what should determine how we value the partnership and what success looks like,” Eisemann said.

A brand’s tiering setup influences how creators are evaluated, how their impact is measured and how they’re utilized in campaigns.

“When expectations, budgets, and measurement rely on different assumptions, effective work can be undervalued, and investment becomes harder to justify or sustain,” said Stefani Stamatiou, CEO of Americas at HYDP creator marketing agency.

But across the industry, there’s no conventional naming system. (Even Digiday staffers couldn’t agree on what defines a creator versus influencer.) And as more money pours into the creator economy, marketers are expecting measurement and business outcomes.

“Greater clarity around inputs and expected outcomes would make it easier to set goals, allocate budgets, and define the role creators play within the broader advertising landscape,” Stamatiou said.

A new product manager role will explore ad-related monetization, suggesting the platform could make ads a bigger part of its business.

CEOs have never cared much for holdcos. Enterprise solutions, on the other hand, get a meeting.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

#7198. eLEND: Mortgage Servicing Analyst

At eLEND, we are committed to delivering an exceptional mortgage experience through innovation, technology, operational excellence, and client-focused partnership. Our Mortgage Servicing team plays a critical role in maintaining accurate loan records, supporting borrowers, managing servicing activities, and ensuring operational consistency throughout the servicing lifecycle.

The Mortgage Servicing Analyst is responsible for executing daily servicing activities, including loan boarding, servicing data maintenance, reconciliations, borrower inquiries, servicing transfers, operational reporting, and exception resolution.

This position works closely with Servicing leadership, Finance, Operations, internal departments, and third-party servicing partners to maintain accurate portfolio information, support regulatory compliance, and ensure servicing activities are completed efficiently and within established procedures.

As a Mortgage Servicing Analyst, you will help maintain the accuracy, integrity, and operational efficiency of eLEND's mortgage servicing portfolio.

While the AVP establishes priorities and monitors overall servicing performance, you will execute the core activities that keep our servicing operations running smoothly.

From boarding new loans and maintaining servicing platform data to researching borrower inquiries, reconciling accounts, and supporting servicing transfers, your work directly contributes to accurate records, timely borrower support, and operational consistency.

Success means maintaining reliable servicing data, resolving routine operational issues, meeting established deadlines, and supporting a compliant and efficient servicing environment.

#7197. Why Qualcomm teamed with F1’s new star in race for sports marketing ROI

The company’s consumer brand Snapdragon signed 20-year-old phenom Kimi Antonelli as a brand ambassador as it looks to connect with racing’s growing audience.

Snapdragon, the consumer brand of chipmaker Qualcomm, on Tuesday launched an anthem film around its partnership with Formula 1 driver Kimi Antonelli. The 20-year-old driver for the Mercedes-AMG Petronas team — of which the brand is also a partner — became the youngest Formula 1 World Champion ever earlier this month.

The video is part of a campaign timed to the 2026 Las Vegas Grand Prix on Nov. 21. It will run on F1 broadcasts outside the U.S. on Sky, on digital and in partnership with channels including Sports Illustrated and YouTube. Apple TV is the exclusive broadcast partner of F1 in the U.S., which prevents brands like Snapdragon from running traditional broadcast ads around the race.

“We've got other ways to bring it to life,” Qualcomm CMO Don McGuire said of the campaign. “We've got creators involved, we've got remixing involved, we have slipstreaming involved… hopefully turning it into an anthem for him that has really long legs.”

A 60-second hero video follows Antonelli during a day at a hotel. Wearing smart glasses and using a tablet, both powered by Snapdragon chips, the phenom listens to a version of the traditional Neapolitan song “Funiculì, Funiculà,” trains in the gym and kicks off a social media trend. The ad ends with a tagline, “Jamme, Jamme. To the top!,” which quotes the song and speaks to his meteoric rise in racing.

“We've been watching Kimi for quite some time, and we have always believed that he was meant for greatness and that he was going to ascend very quickly. I think we made the right bet,” McGuire said.

Snapdragon’s partnership with Antonelli builds on the brand’s deal with Mercedes-AMG Petronas, a strategic collaboration that kicked off in 2023. Qualcomm had previously partnered with the team in the 2010s, a deal that was not “ROI-driven, marketing-driven,” said McGuire, who became CMO in 2021. The new partnership looks to better connect the brand’s objectives with the growing audience of F1, both in the U.S. and globally.

“The sport more and more is not something that I can judge based on [advertising value equivalency]. We have some pretty good placements on the Mercedes cars, especially with cockpit cameras and things like that, but it's just so fleeting, from a visibility standpoint,” McGuire said.

Those issues are exacerbated by the Apple TV deal, which limits brands’ ability to measure impact during broadcasts. Qualcomm is committed to F1, Mercedes-AMG Petronas and Antonelli, especially as it seeks to bring the partnership from content and storytelling through activations and hospitality.

“We believe we're already seeing higher ROI coming out of some things that we've done in the short term,” McGuire said. “We believe in the sport, why we were there and the fundamentals.”

Outside of F1, Snapdragon is no stranger to sports. Since the start of the 2024-25 season, the brand has been the principal shirt sponsor of Manchester United. Despite the iconic soccer team’s on- and off-field issues in recent years, the deal has yielded “humongous ROI” for Snapdragon, McGuire said.

“When you're dealing with a sports brand that big, at scale, the brand can withstand the ebbs and flows of performance on the pitch because the fan base is so huge,” the executive explained. “That's the benchmark that we use [for sports partnerships] and maybe it's a little unfair because they are arguably the biggest sports brand in the world.”

Snapdragon’s latest F1 marketing comes on the heels of a holiday campaign that launched earlier this month. At the heart of “The Gift That Changes Everything” is a sweet 60-second hero spot that shows how a mom and dad make Christmas seem to come sooner. The parents use a Snapdragon-powered PC to learn how to build a "time machine" that gives their child a special Christmas morning experience.

The campaign is running across broadcast, connected TV and paid social in the U.S. with CTV and paid social in the U.K., Germany and France. Created in partnership with 72andSunny New York and 72andSunny Toronto, the effort iterates on a “Snapdragon. That’s How” creative platform that launched earlier this year. The campaign has seen strong preliminary results, McGuire reported.

The holiday creative arrives as the brand prepares for a season that could see hardware makers impacted by memory shortages, reducing Snapdragon’s total addressable market at a time when the Nvidia scandal is roiling the chip market. The brand continues to work to build awareness and affinity, regardless of short-term market conditions.

“This is about building muscle and emotion that can sustain the long-term,” McGuire said. “We feel really strongly that we had to lean into holiday as if it were any other holiday season, regardless of the macro conditions.”

#7196. KERV.ai, Macmillan Publishers and Chomps are among this year’s Work/Life Awards finalists

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This year’s Work/Life Awards finalists reflect a landscape where companies are rethinking workplace culture as a driver of business performance, employee engagement and innovation. Across industries, organizations are giving employees a more active role in shaping workplace policies and priorities, building recognition and connection into the everyday employee experience and extending their missions through community involvement and social impact.

KERV.ai earned a finalist spot in the Most Innovative Culture category for making employee input and cross-functional collaboration central to its workplace culture. Its quarterly HR listening tours, culture committee and real-time feedback programs gave employees opportunities to influence company policies and engagement initiatives. The emphasis on employee voice also extended to the business. Employees across product, engineering, client services and creative identified an opportunity to improve contextual targeting, leading to the development of Moment Match Engine, a video technology solution built through cross-departmental collaboration.

Macmillan Publishers is nominated in the Most Committed to Social Good category for connecting its workplace culture to a broader mission of expanding access to books, literacy and intellectual freedom. Through its MacImpact corporate social responsibility program, the publisher supports employee volunteering, charitable giving and community partnerships. In 2025, employees took part in 27 volunteer events and contributed 494 volunteer hours, a 60% increase from the previous year. Its employee-led Yours to Read Committee also advocates for access to diverse literature and the freedom to read.

Chomps, a finalist for Most Committed to Employee Appreciation, focused on maintaining employee connection as its remote-first workforce grew to more than 200 people across 30-plus states. The company brings employees together through annual retreats, departmental offsites and company-wide gatherings, while recognition programs such as Chompian of the Quarter and Chompian of the Year celebrate employee contributions. Investments in wellness, mental health days and professional development further extend appreciation beyond formal recognition.

Together, this year’s nominees represent a workplace landscape in which culture is shaped by employee participation, meaningful recognition and a commitment to a broader purpose beyond business goals. As expectations of employers continue to evolve, their approaches offer examples of how organizations can build more connected, engaged and purpose-driven workplaces.

#7193. Growth Copywriter at Winona

Winona is one of the leading telemedicine companies providing HRT for women in menopause. We’ve built all of our technology in house including an EHR, patient care admin system, patient portal, website, marketing technology etc. Winona has two compounding pharmacies and a team of in house physicians providing world-class care via a fully vertically integrated business model.

We are looking for a data-backed, empathetic Growth Copywriter to own the performance and optimization of our end-to-end messaging ecosystem. At Winona, copy isn’t just about sounding nice. It’s a measurable variable in our growth funnel. You will design and execute rapid copy experiments across our paid acquisition, intake flows, and lifecycle retention loops to help women confidently access bioidentical HRT and menopause care.

Full-Funnel Experimentation. Write copy hypotheses, design rapid A/B tests alongside growth and analytics teams, analyze performance data, and turn winning variations into a scalable messaging playbook.

Lifecycle Growth & Retention. Write, test, and optimize automated onboarding, activation, adherence, churn-save, and win-back sequences across email, SMS, and push notifications to drive LTV.

High-Intent Conversion Surfaces. Own the persuasive strategy and microcopy for landing pages, interactive quiz/intake flows, checkouts, and dynamic pricing/offer messaging.

Paid Acquisition at Scale. Generate volume-driven concepts, hooks, and static/video copy variants for Meta, Google, and YouTube, systematically iterating based on creative testing data.

Voice of Customer (VoC) Intelligence. Continuously mine product reviews, customer support tickets, intake data, and exit surveys to isolate deep qualitative insights and high-converting customer language.

AI-Augmented Workflows. Leverage and train Winona’s internal AI copywriting tools to scale production velocity, maintain brand consistency, and build automated variant pipelines without sacrificing quality.

5+ years of dedicated copywriting experience specializing in direct-response, conversion copywriting, or growth marketing.

DTC Telehealth, Wellness, or HealthTech experience is highly preferred. You understand how to write about sensitive health topics with clinical authority and deep empathy.

A portfolio backed by data and results — the exact copy variants you tested, the structural or psychological changes you made, and the quantifiable lift they produced in metrics like CTR, quiz completion, or conversion rates.

#7180. NowThis Acquires Impact Media in ‘Seven-Figure’ Deal to Reach Younger, Civic-Minded Audiences

The social video publisher NowThis has acquired Impact Media, the Gen Z-led company behind the social accounts @impact and @environment, in a seven-figure, all-cash deal, according to NowThis CEO Sharon Mussalli. Further terms were not disclosed.

#7175. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Ship your first production change to a live agent through the team's review process, and be able to explain how a member's call flows through the Agents Platform end to end

Learn one agent deeply: system prompt, tool contracts, orchestration triggers, compliance gates, and the implementation process from kickoff to go-live

Join at least one credit union implementation as the second engineer, including a live testing session with their staff

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Own the technical side of one credit union implementation end to end: core and LOS integration, agent configuration, testing, go-live, and the first weeks of production

Run the eval and tuning loop for your agent (golden transcripts, rubric judges, regression suite) and be trusted to change its behavior without a second engineer checking your work

Have completed at least two on-site visits and turned what you heard there into product decisions with your pod lead and PM

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

Be the engineer credit union staff ask for by name when something in their deployment needs to change

Have written the implementation playbook for your agent well enough that the AI Ops team runs the second and third credit union without you

Be shaping what the next agents look like, because you have seen where the current ones break in the field

7+ years of software engineering in a fast-paced environment, preferably in SaaS and/or Fintech, with senior TypeScript and Node.js backend experience in a large codebase (NestJS, CQRS, schema-validated boundaries, dependency injection). We do not count years of AI experience; the field is too young for that to mean anything. We look at what you have shipped and how you reason about it

Integration and API engineering: secure service-to-service endpoints, third-party APIs, webhooks, and the patience to work with legacy banking systems that were not designed to be integrated

Security and auth judgment: OAuth and machine-to-machine auth, tenant-scoped access, secrets handling, no PII in logs. Our agents touch sensitive member data and operate under consumer-contact regulation

Customer presence: you can sit across from a VP of Collections, hear a request, and say "here is what I recommend and why", including when the answer is no. You are comfortable making calls in the room and owning them afterwards

Code-quality judgment under deadline pressure: you take on debt deliberately, name it, and pay it down, rather than gold-plating or leaving silent debt behind

Genuine appetite for LLM agents: eval discipline for non-deterministic systems, reading a system prompt like code, understanding that some decisions must never reach the model. Curiosity plus shipped work is enough; research depth is not required

Nice to have, explicitly not required: voice and telephony (LiveKit, Twilio), Temporal or event-driven orchestration, Python, core banking or LOS integration experience, credit union or banking domain knowledge

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

You'll decide how our GTM stack is designed, what we build versus buy, and where we invest next — and you'll be hands-on building it: Salesforce architecture, integrations, enrichment pipelines, and the agentic workflows that help Sales and Marketing move faster and smarter.

Salesforce is our system of record for revenue, pipeline, and customer data. HubSpot is our marketing layer for campaigns and lead generation, and what it captures needs to flow cleanly and reliably into Salesforce. Around them sits a growing stack of enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and data platform tools. You'll own the architecture that ties it all together: disciplined change management, observable pipelines, data models built for AI consumption, and agentic workflows that act on GTM signals at scale — designed to serve the business for years, not quarters.

This role sits at the intersection of GTM, Data, Finance, and Engineering. You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

#7165. How Elex Michaelson Fills CNN’s West Coast Gap

Elex Michaelson got a firsthand look at CNN journalists working a breaking news situation during the White House Correspondents’ Dinner in April.

Michaelson was one of the network’s guests that evening at the Washington Hilton, in Washington, D.C. He described being there as a “wonderful experience,” but it took a sharp turn when shots rang out within the building, and the president was quickly evacuated.

“The shooting itself was a very strange experience, and to see everybody get to work in their tuxes and trying to figure out what happened was also something I’ll never forget,” said Michaelson.

The Los Angeles-based anchor of CNN’s late-night program, The Story Is, airing from 12 to 2 a.m. ET, Michaelson is no stranger to handling breaking news situations. He broke two major international news stories this year: he was the first to report when the US undertook military strikes and captured Nicolás Maduro in Venezuela in January, and he was also the first to report on the Iran bombings in March, both of which, coincidentally, happened around the same time on a Friday night at around 10:30 p.m. PT/1:30 a.m. ET. This past Friday, he was on the air for four hours as part of CNN’s continuous coverage of the FAA issuing a ground stop for all the planes across the entire country.

Speaking to TVNewser, Michaelson said, “CNN does a lot of things well, but I think it does breaking news best of all.”

For the Venezuelan military strikes, Michaelson recalls how they were covering a pop culture story when a producer told him over his earpiece that there were explosions in Caracas. In that moment, he realized who he was working for.

“We said goodbye to the people, to a quick commercial break reset, and then we were off, and it was a reminder of just the global power of CNN.”

Michaelson joined CNN in September 2025, bringing his West Coast sensibilities to the network. Before that, he was an anchor for the LA-based Fox affiliate KTTV 11 and earlier worked as a reporter at KABC-TV in Los Angeles and as a weekend morning anchor and weeknight reporter for XETV in San Diego (when the Tijuana-based station had English-language operations and a news department there). This past October, he and his team celebrated the first anniversary of The Story Is, which he describes as serving a niche that didn’t exist before: an audience in the western part of the U.S. that didn’t have a live news program airing during primetime hours in that region.

“I think we’ve filled that gap,” he said. Adding, “I think we’ve also brought some fresh voices that you don’t see on cable news all day long that have a lot to say and are a valuable addition to the conversation.”

In its first year, according to CNN, The Story Is with Elex Michaelson has seen its total viewer audience grow by 15% since launch, compared with the six-month pre-launch period. It also led cable news among adults 25–54 when news broke of U.S. strikes on Venezuela on January 2, 2026 (1:45 – 2 a.m. ET). The show now attracts nearly one in four viewers in the Adults 25–54 demo in its Pacific time slot, up from one in five before launch, and is No. 1 in cable news among Hispanic viewers in its time slot, both in total viewers and the demo.

The Story Is goes beyond politics, covering the news with a West Coast, laid-back feel. Michaelson said CNN president Mark Thompson described the program as “the most upbeat show on CNN,” and the anchor pointed out that during those hours they have had thoughtful entertainment conversations, profiled athletes involved in their communities, and shared other community service stories.

“I love politics, but I also am particularly proud of some of these profile stories that we’ve done with celebrities,” Michaelson said. “I really love the sit-down we did with William Shatner and his daughter, both of whom had just battled stage four cancer and survived it.”

He touted the advantage of anchoring out of CNN’s LA bureau, which lets him showcase those types of stories. “If I was in Washington, you’re competing with like a thousand other reporters doing the same thing, on Capitol Hill, for example, but in this space there is more room to do something unique, which has been fun.”

As for the show’s first anniversary, amid the busy news cycle, Michaelson brought in pizza and toasted with champagne after the show to mark the occasion. He hopes to have a proper celebration in the near future now that the midterm elections have concluded.

Mark Mwachiro is a TVNewser contributor who has been writing for the blog since March 2022. Based out of New York, Mark has also contributed to ADWEEK.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Fox News was the No. 1 network in total viewers, and CNN led the way in the demo

#7155. Disney Posts Blockbuster Q4 Boosted by Sports and Marvel Comics Universe

16% — Walt Disney Co. revenue growth for the fiscal fourth quarter ended Oct. 3, inflated more than 7 percentage points by a 53rd week in the current fiscal year. Earnings per share also grew 16%, even adjusting out that 53rd week effect.

$3 billion — Total annual cost-cutting savings run rate Disney expect by fiscal 2029 through layoffs, procurement and other efficiencies.

24% — Increase in aggregated viewership of Monday Night Football through the first nine weeks of the NFL season, a good sign heading into Disney’s first-ever Super Bowl broadcast.

For a company anticipating significant layoffs over the next two years, there was a lot of good news.

The September Ohio State-Texas college football broadcast on ESPN drew 14 million viewers, ESPN’s biggest audience for a college game in 10 years, per Disney’s shareholder letter. U.S. Open viewership came in at four-year highs.

Disney CEO Josh D’Amaro expects more big things from the Marvel Comics Universe with Avengers Doomsday opening next month, building on the theatrical momentum of summer’s Spider-Man: Brand New Day and the best month ever for MCU viewership on Disney+ in August, he said on the company’s earnings call.

Disney+ also tapped a South Korean creative pipeline once dominated by Netflix with The Remarried Empress, which on Nov. 3 had the biggest premiere ever for a local original across Disney+ and Hulu globally, D’Amaro said: “We haven’t even begun to see the benefits of our investment in international content.”

Next month, Disney+ will begin incorporating TikTok creator content as part of its agreement with that platform, D’Amaro said, adding that the company is continuing to explore the option of a Disney+ free tier “to reach a more price-sensitive consumer.”

Expansion of the NCAA College Football Playoff to 24 teams, and from 11 to 23 games next year, promises an even bigger sports rights earnings take for Disney. “ESPN is going to have exclusive rights to all the rounds,” D’Amaro said. “We’ve got broad distribution flexibility across ABC and ESPN.”

The CEO declined to disclose the rights costs, and acknowledged that Disney has sub-licensing rights, but didn’t commit to any plans to get outside networks involved.

“Changes like these are not easy, and they affect people who care deeply about Disney and have contributed so much to this company. At the same time, these steps are important if we want to be a company that moves faster, adapts to a rapidly changing environment, and keeps investing in the content and technology that will drive our growth. We make these choices because we believe so strongly in what Disney can be.” —Disney CEO Josh D’Amaro on impending layoffs

#7147. A Crash Course in Marketing to Machines

Some years ago, one of us, Jim Lecinski, wrote a manifesto called the “Zero Moment of Truth,” or “ZMOT,” which argued that before the traditional point of purchase arrives — before a customer reaches the shampoo aisle shelf or the car dealer’s lot -— that customer goes looking for evidence to help them decide what products are the most credible and therefore what to buy.

Many marketers still begin the marketing process from the company out: Who are we? What do we want to sell? What message can we create to persuade people to buy? In effect, they are working their way from the product to the customer. Great marketers, however, work the other way around.

They begin by asking what job the customer is trying to get done, how that customer moves through the purchase journey, what questions arise along the way, and which sources shape confidence and choice. Then they align their go-to-market accordingly.

Digital marketing has made the customer decision process more complex. Rather than relying on a single message or source, customers consult multiple sources before deciding what to buy. They gather evidence. They search, compare, read what other customers say, watch experts and creators, and increasingly ask AI assistants for answers and recommendations.

The original three sources – search engines, social conversations, and ratings and reviews – remain important. But two others now deserve a place in the ZMOT framework: content creators and AI assistants. Both influence how customers evaluate choices, form preferences, and make purchase decisions.

And these sources also interact. Search results show online community discussions, reviews, and creator videos. Creator content influences people and AI-generated answers. AI assistants draw from information created across the web, synthesize it, and increasingly build or narrow the consideration set on the customer’s behalf.

The Zero Moment of Truth provides the clearest framework for understanding this change.

The concept of the Zero Moment remains as relevant today as when it was introduced: The Zero Moment of Truth is the critical pre-purchase period when customers seek information, compare options, reduce uncertainty, and form preferences. A stimulus creates awareness or activates a need. Before the traditional point of purchase arrives, the customer goes looking for evidence that will help them decide what to believe and what to choose.

What continues to evolve are the sources customers use to gather the information that shapes this decision-making zero moment.

Content Creators help customers understand categories, interpret differences, see products in context, and decide with confidence. AI assistants increasingly answer questions, compare alternatives, form shortlists, analyze options, and recommend specific brands.

The expansion of avenues from which customers acquire knowledge, requires marketers to rethink their approach. A customer considering a new suitcase may move among search, online community discussions, ratings & reviews, content creator videos, and an AI chatbot all during a single decision journey. These five sources therefore form an interconnected portfolio rather than separate or sequential channels.

Therefore, winning the Zero Moment still requires clear and persuasive brand messages across these various avenues. The marketer’s mandate now extends beyond the messages the brand creates and distributes. The expanded Zero Moment requires marketers to move from managing brand messages alone to managing the broader body of decision evidence that customers and AI systems use to understand, evaluate, and choose brands. Each source has a distinct role:

These roles overlap, which is precisely why marketers must manage them together. Creator videos appear in search results. Online community conversations influence both human perceptions and AI-generated answers. Reviews shape search visibility, customer trust, and machine-generated comparisons. Brand content can support every source when it is useful, clear, current, and corroborated by evidence outside the brand.

Notice that AI assistants differ from the other four sources because they play two roles. They are a fifth source customers consult directly, and they synthesize information drawn from the other four. This becomes even more important as AI is directly embedded across categories, including social, ecommerce and productivity.

An AI-generated answer may incorporate search results, brand websites, publishers, social conversations, reviews, creator content, and other third-party evidence. AI increasingly assembles and interprets the consideration set on the customer’s behalf. It can identify contradictions, surface recurring criticisms, compare claims, and omit brands that lack sufficient credible evidence from the answers or recommendations it provides. In fact, Google recently introduced new ad formats including Conversational Discovery Ads, Direct Offers, and Highlighted Ads, which answer questions, supply analysis and offer suggestions right inside the ad creative.

Strong search rankings no longer guarantee visibility in AI-generated answers. A brand may perform well in search engines but be missing from the recommendations from AI assistants or overviews. It may have a polished website while customers, creators, and reviewers tell a different story elsewhere. It may appear across all five sources with inconsistent product information, claims, pricing, or positioning.

Each source can strengthen or weaken the evidence found in the others.

Marketers still need persuasive messages, which are an essential part of the evidence customers encounter.

Marketers have traditionally focused on the messages they create and distribute. Today, they also must understand and influence the evidence customers encounter elsewhere, including reviews and creator content and AI-generated recommendations.

Decision evidence is the accumulated body of information, experiences, claims, reviews, demonstrations, conversations, and third-party validation that shapes what customers and AI systems understand and believe about a brand.

This evidence includes useful and authoritative brand content, accurate product information, strong customer experiences, current ratings and reviews, credible third-party coverage, informed creator content, and consistent facts that both humans and machines can understand. YouTube becomes a valuable source of authority, based on the way large language models prioritize multimodal content and direct video citations. A recent BrightEdge report indicates that a brand’s YouTube presence represents a unique path to AI optimization. As the dominant video platform in AI search, YouTube is already capturing nearly 30% of AI citations in Google’s AI Overviews (source).

While brands cannot fully control every source they can influence the quality, availability, credibility, and coherence of the evidence customers encounter. The goal is to ensure that the evidence available across all five sources supports the position the brand wants to earn in the customer’s mind.

To help marketers see how effectively their brand appears during the Zero Moment of Truth we’ve developed a helpful Zero Moment Five-Source Evidence Check.

Start with the three to five questions that matter most in the customer’s decision. These might include:

Then examine what a customer would find about the brand across search engines, social conversations, ratings and reviews, content creators, and AI assistants. For each source, assess five things:

Record your brand’s results for each source as Strong, Weak, or Absent (See Fig 2). The weak and absent cells reveal the most important evidence gaps.

With the completed diagnostic in hand, the next step is to close the gaps. Prioritize the weak or absent areas most likely to influence customer choice, assign clear owners and actions, and strengthen the underlying evidence through better content, corrected information, stronger customer experiences, review management, creator engagement, or credible third-party validation.

Then repeat the check regularly. The goal is to move every priority area toward Strong and keep it there as customer behavior, platform results, and AI-generated answers continue to change.

The expanded Zero Moment requires marketers to move from managing brand messages alone to managing the decision evidence customers and AI systems use to understand, evaluate, and choose brands.

The challenge for marketers is no longer about getting the right message in front of the right customer. It’s ensuring that when customers – or AI agents – look for evidence, they find information that is accurate and credible while also maintaining a consistent brand promise.

Jim Lecinski is a Clinical Professor of Marketing at Northwestern University's Kellogg School of Management where he has won multiple prestigious teaching awards including Kellogg Professor of the Year. His career includes twelve years at Google, where he was vp of customer solutions for the Americas. Lecinski's seminal book “Winning the Zero Moment of Truth” has been read by over 300,000 marketers, and his latest project, “The AI Marketing Canvas: A Five Step AI Plan for Marketers” has been named both a top AI book and a top business book.

Kelly Cutler brings together industry experience and academic expertise in her role as Clinical Associate Professor at Northwestern University, where she teaches marketing and communication across multiple schools and programs. She also serves as Associate Director of Research at the Spiegel Research Center, focusing on AI integration and the evolving digital marketing landscape. Kelly is regularly featured in both academic and industry publications and speaks at conferences and events around the world. She remains actively engaged with industry, advising organizations and delivering executive education and professional development programs to global audiences.

#7139. Amazon’s New Christmas Ad Is a Bet Against Itself

Editor’s note: Brave Bison, which acquired Mark Ritson’s MiniMBA in 2025, owns about 28% of System1 and sought to acquire the rest this year.

Joy is stuck at the airport on Christmas Eve, the snow coming down hard, and every flight cancelled. So she picks up her bag of presents and sets off for home the long way: a bus, a lift from some donkey farmers, a night in a boat cabin on a heaving sea, a taxi driven by a giant. At every stop, she thanks a stranger with one of the Amazon parcels meant for her family.

This is “Joy Delivered”, Amazon’s new holiday ad, which launched earlier this month and runs like a proper movie. A new version of the Beatles’ “Golden Slumbers” does a lot of the emotional heavy lifting and the hug at the end will get you.

There are two problems with the ad, however. One is peripheral. One central.

The peripheral one first. Amazon filmed its white Christmas in New Zealand, in and around Dunedin, to be specific. But it’s summer down there, and it will be even warmer by Christmas, when most of the country will be at the beach in a pair of shorts.

The central problem is bigger. Amazon already has a great Christmas ad: “Joy Ride.”

Three old ladies sitting on a snowy hill watch the local kids go sledding. One of them orders some seat cushions from Amazon. The box turns up and the next thing you know they are flying down the hill and, for a few seconds, they are girls again. There are no words. Just an instrumental of “In My Life.”

“Joy Ride” first ran in 2023 and got the maximum 5.8 stars from System1. Basically perfection.

Then Amazon did something very unusual for a big marketer. Last year, instead of making a new ad, it ran the old one again. It scored 5.7, again putting it in the rare company of less than 1% of all ads made all year.

None of this should surprise anyone. Coca-Cola first ran “Holidays Are Coming” in 1995. The trucks have rolled out every Christmas since, and System1 says the ad always scores five stars in its testing. When System1 looked at more than 50,000 ads, it found that scores barely move as ads get older, and that some of the very best actually improve as people get to know them.

Ads don’t wear out, at least not for consumers.

Amazon’s problem is that its advertising decisions are made by marketers — people who like to make new, shiny stuff. Jo Shoesmith, Amazon’s global chief brand officer, told ADWEEK that people want nostalgia but also “something they haven’t seen before.”

The average brand building campaign reaches its maximum impact about two to three years into execution. Seasonal stuff can often, as Coke has proved, live on for decades. But the average marketer is tired of their ad, and wants to make something new.

It’s the same story with slogans, with logos, with distinctive brand assets. Marketers forget they are not the consumer. They look at their ads for at least 9,000 hours before they release it into the wild, and are over it before most of the target market has noticed it.

There is an intermediate position. Amazon could pull a Coca-Cola: bring out their old ad every year but also make a crappy, inferior new execution alongside it. But why create a bat to beat yourself with? Better to remove the outstanding, already made, already free, already proven, already much-loved Christmas ad completely, and then run something less good, with lots more risk and cost attached. Makes perfect sense.

We read all the time that marketers need more support from their CEO, more budget, more seats in the boardroom.

But what they mostly need is patience. It takes years, not months, for a good campaign to work. Amazon knew that last Christmas, but it has already forgotten it.

Mark Ritson has a PhD in Marketing and spent 25 years working as a marketing professor, and has also worked as both a global brand consultant and as the in-house brand consultant for LVMH. His articles have appeared in the Sloan Management Review, Harvard Business Review, the Journal of Advertising and the Journal of Consumer Research.

And why it can't just be jumping on the Grand Theft Auto bandwagon

#7137. Technical Account Manager (APJ) at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re seeking a Technical Account Manager (TAM) to be the primary technical partner for our Team & customers after the sale, helping them adopt, operate, and get long-term value from Supabase.

At Supabase, TAMs are the technical owners of their assigned accounts, working closely with our most valuable customers to:

Understand each customer’s architecture, use cases, constraints, and goals.

Validate deployments and guide customers through onboarding and early success.

Proactively surface risks and optimization opportunities before they become incidents.

Coordinate internal teams on complex escalations and drive root cause analysis.

Align Supabase capabilities with the customer’s roadmap.

Successful TAMs are proactive, technically credible, and customer-first. TAM is a relationship, not a response channel: you’ll act as an extension of your customers’ teams and advocate on their behalf within Supabase.

You’ll be joining our Success team, where you will be expected to lead and innovate around the following areas of responsibility:

Act as the technical owner for your assigned accounts, documenting environments, mapping dependencies, and tracking technical risks.

Validate architecture and deployment plans after the sale, guide initial configuration and best practices, and make sure customers understand platform capabilities and limitations.

Help customers reach early success milestones to shorten time to value and reduce early friction.

Regularly review customer environments and usage patterns to identify scalability, performance, reliability, security, and compliance risks and opportunities.

Plan upgrades and lifecycle events, and run architecture reviews for upcoming initiatives, including capacity and growth planning, feature readiness, and early identification of technical blockers.

Coordinate across Support and Engineering on complex or high-impact issues. TAMs are not frontline support, but provide the context, prioritization, and follow-through that drive resolution, root cause analysis, and preventative action.

Serve as the voice of the customer to Engineering and Product, sharing specific, reproducible feedback on recurring pain points and taking part in beta and early access programs.

Partner with Sales and Customer Success on account health and retention, with technical input on renewals, expansions, and evaluations, and early flags on risks to satisfaction.

6+ years of relevant work experience in engineering, SRE, technical consulting, or similar, including operating production systems under real constraints.

Strong technical depth across database internals, scalability, security posture, and infrastructure tradeoffs. You understand a customer’s architecture as well as they do, without escalating every question.

A proactive mindset: you spot risks and optimization opportunities before the customer does, and you treat your accounts like a personal portfolio.

Sound escalation judgment: you know when to escalate, when to resolve internally, and how to communicate clearly under pressure without creating noise.

Very strong communication skills. TAMs explain complex issues to non-technical executives and root causes to senior engineers, often in the same day.

Awareness of how TAM performance shows up in retention and NRR, including surfacing expansion signals and renewal risks early.

Experience working in async or globally distributed teams.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

Most Kit creators don't have a dedicated Creator Growth Manager, and when their emails start landing in spam, they need someone who can find the cause quickly and explain it in plain language. We're looking for a Deliverability and Compliance Specialist to own deliverability support for that long tail of creators, run Kit's abuse desk, and make the compliance calls that protect the sending reputation every creator on Kit shares.

You'll also build the playbooks, training, and AI-assisted workflows that help Support and contractors handle more deliverability questions on their own, and you'll partner with our infrastructure engineers as we run more of our sending in-house. This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes digging through headers and bounce logs to find the real cause of a problem, can explain DMARC alignment to a first-time creator without jargon, and is comfortable making a firm call when a situation is gray. Our Deliverability team: a Deliverability Lead who supports our highest-value accounts and leads deliverability education, and a Deliverability Specialist who onboards and ramps up large senders moving to Kit

Our infrastructure engineers, who run Kit's sending infrastructure

Our compliance and anti-abuse engineers, who build the automated checks that keep spammers off Kit

Product Specialists and the Support team, who handle the front line and escalate deliverability issues to you

Customer Success Managers (we call them Creator Growth Managers at Kit), Sales and Migrations, and Product Managers

First Month: Shadow the Deliverability team and Product Specialists on deliverability tickets, account approvals, and abuse reports, then start answering queue tickets with review. Join the daily monitoring rotation (blocklist delisting, Postmaster and delivery monitoring) and the weekly Deliverability team meeting. Learn how account approvals, compliance flags, and our in-house sending platform work. By the end of the month, work deliverability tickets and abuse reports on your own and send your first delisting requests. Project: build a recurring report showing the trending deliverability ticket reasons so we understand what’s causing how to reduce friction for customers and how to solve it

Be the go-to contact for day-to-day mailbox provider issues with Microsoft, Gmail, Yahoo, Comcast, and regional providers, and write an incident runbook for blocklistings and provider throttling. Track the queue with a simple scorecard (volume, first response time, and escalation rate) and share what you learn with the team. You're helping shape the internal tooling and alerting our in-house sending needs, and you take part in industry groups like M3AAWG.

#7101. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Owning a data domain over time and evolving its models as the business and its questions changed.

Working at a SaaS company, ideally one with both a self-serve and a sales-led motion. Commerce, payments, or creator economy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Working on a small or remote team where you were trusted to make decisions independently.

Ownership and follow-through. You treat your data domains as yours. You notice problems before stakeholders do, close the loop on requests, and finish what you start.

Judgment. You match rigor to the decision at hand, know when a quick answer is enough and when a request should become a durable model, and say so clearly.

Flexibility. You're glad to step outside your primary area when another team needs help, and you balance that work against your core commitments openly.

Teach everything you know. You document as you go, pair generously, and help stakeholders understand the data well enough to use it on their own.

Work in public. You share progress, open questions, and decisions in writing where the team can see them, including what didn't work.

Creator success is our success. You remember who's on the other end. A wrong number in a pipeline review means Sales spends time on the wrong creators, and that shapes how carefully you work.

Do it with excellence. You test your work, review it carefully, and don't ship a number you can't defend.

Candor with care. You're comfortable pushing back when a request won't answer the real question, and you do it in a way that builds trust.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Discomfort with ambiguity. Some of our sales and commerce definitions are still evolving, and you'll help settle them.

Needing several layers of approval to make modeling decisions within your domain.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

Wanting a strictly fixed scope. Sales comes first, but you'll pick up Product or other work when the business needs it.

Wanting mostly greenfield work. Much of this role is improving sales models and dashboards that already exist.

Preferring to take tickets as written rather than partnering with stakeholders on what they need.

Being skeptical of AI's capabilities, or using AI without good judgment and accountability.

Expecting a large data team with dedicated specialists for every part of the stack.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time - so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.
